If I’m not driving or doing something, I get kind of antsy. I’ll start making extra work for myself, like redoing route plans, checking in on new drivers after hours, or fixing little things around the house. Sitting still for too long just doesn’t feel right lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself acting on impulse more often lately, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve been doing anything too out of character or reckless. Maybe I’ll speak before I think sometimes, but I haven’t noticed myself making big impulsive choices.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus on tasks that require mental effort, like reading or filling out paperwork?\n\nPatient: Yeah, especially with the paperwork side of things. My mind just keeps jumping around. I’ll be trying to look at a delivery note or remember who I said I’d call back, and then some little thing catches my attention — a CB chirp, a fuel price sign, or just some new thought — and I lose the thread. It’s stuff I normally keep track of pretty well, so it’s been frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members since you started experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been affecting things some. At work I’ve had dispatch and a couple clients repeat details I should’ve caught the first time, and I’ve missed a few sales follow-ups because I was trying to juggle too many ideas at once.\n\nAt home, my wife and daughter seem like they’re kind of walking on eggshells around me. I can tell I’m adding stress when I’m supposed to be helping, and that doesn’t feel good.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or fidgety than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being especially fidgety compared to other kids. I was probably a normal active kid, but nothing that stands out to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I got through high school okay. I’m sure I had times where I didn’t listen perfectly, like any kid, but nothing that stands out as a real issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get wrapped up in something for a bit, but I don’t think I tune everything else out in any unusual way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on a task that you lost track of time?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, if I’m doing something I’m interested in, time can pass like it does for anyone, but I wouldn’t say I lose track of time in a big way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so engrossed in an activity that you found it difficult to stop or switch to something else?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can usually stop what I’m doing if I need to. I mean, if I’m doing something I’m interested in, time can pass like it does for anyone, but I wouldn’t say I lose track of time in a big way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so engrossed in an activity that you found it difficult to stop or switch to something else?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can usually stop what I’m doing if I need to. I might want to finish something, but it’s not like I can’t switch gears.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself intensely focused on a task to the point where you ignore everything else around you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can concentrate on something if I need to, but I’m still aware of what’s going on around me. It’s not like I block everything out.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or behaving impulsively when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t remember having big problems with that as a kid. I mean, I probably got distracted sometimes like anybody, but nothing that really stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on you having trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I don’t recall teachers or my parents making a big deal about me not paying attention or not sitting still. If they did, it didn’t stick with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as mind jumping around or getting distracted easily, when you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember that being a big issue for me as a teenager. I mean, I probably got distracted here and there, like anyone, but nothing that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any symptoms like these when you were a child, such as difficulty paying attention or getting easily distracted?\n\nPatient: As a child, not really that I remember. The attention problem I’m talking about is more recent—these past months my mind keeps jumping to every CB chirp, fuel price sign, or some new idea, and then I miss details I normally wouldn’t, like a delivery note or who I said I’d call back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
